package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here

public class CalculatorTest {
    private final Calculator calculator = new Calculator();

    @Test
    public void test_add_positive_integers() {
        // If the method add is called with two positive integers (e.g., 2 and 3), then it must return their arithmetic sum (5). This verifies the basic functional requirement of summing positive numbers.
        int result = calculator.add(2, 3);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_negative_integers() {
        // If the method add is called with two negative integers (e.g., -2 and -3), then it must return their arithmetic sum (-5). This verifies that the addition logic correctly handles negative operands.
        int result = calculator.add(-2, -3);
        assertEquals(-5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_mixed_sign_positive_first() {
        // If the method add is called with a positive integer as the first argument and a negative integer as the second (e.g., 5 and -3), then it must return the correct arithmetic sum (2). This tests addition involving mixed signs where the positive operand is larger in magnitude.
        int result = calculator.add(5, -3);
        assertEquals(2, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_mixed_sign_negative_first() {
        // If the method add is called with a negative integer as the first argument and a positive integer as the second (e.g., -5 and 3), then it must return the correct arithmetic sum (-2). This tests addition involving mixed signs where the negative operand is larger in magnitude.
        int result = calculator.add(-5, 3);
        assertEquals(-2, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_zero_to_positive() {
        // If the method add is called with a positive integer and zero (e.g., 5 and 0), then it must return the positive integer unchanged (5). This verifies the identity property of addition with zero.
        int result = calculator.add(5, 0);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_zero_to_negative() {
        // If the method add is called with a negative integer and zero (e.g., -5 and 0), then it must return the negative integer unchanged (-5). This verifies the identity property of addition with zero for negative numbers.
        int result = calculator.add(-5, 0);
        assertEquals(-5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_zero_to_zero() {
        // If the method add is called with zero and zero (0 and 0), then it must return 0. This verifies the base case of adding two zeros.
        int result = calculator.add(0, 0);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_max_value_overflow() {
        // If the method add is called with Integer.MAX_VALUE and 1, then it must return Integer.MIN_VALUE. This verifies that integer overflow wraps around correctly according to standard Java int arithmetic, as no exception is specified in the documentation.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_min_value_underflow() {
        // If the method add is called with Integer.MIN_VALUE and -1, then it must return Integer.MAX_VALUE. This verifies that integer underflow wraps around correctly according to standard Java int arithmetic, as no exception is specified in the documentation.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, -1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_extreme_values() {
        // If the method add is called with Integer.MAX_VALUE and Integer.MIN_VALUE, then it must return -1. This verifies correct arithmetic handling at the boundaries of the integer range.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE);
        assertEquals(-1, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_commutative_property() {
        // If the method add is called with arguments (a, b) and separately with (b, a) for any valid integers (e.g., 10 and 20), then both calls must return the same result (30). This verifies that the operation is commutative, a fundamental property of addition.
        int result1 = calculator.add(10, 20);
        int result2 = calculator.add(20, 10);
        assertEquals(result1, result2);
        assertEquals(30, result1);
    }


}
package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here
public class CalculatorTest {

    private final Calculator calculator = new Calculator();

    @Test
    public void test_add_positive_integers() {
        // If the method add is called with two positive integers (e.g., 2 and 3), then it must return their arithmetic sum (5). This verifies the basic functional requirement of summing positive numbers.
        int result = calculator.add(2, 3);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_negative_integers() {
        // If the method add is called with two negative integers (e.g., -2 and -3), then it must return their arithmetic sum (-5). This verifies that the addition logic correctly handles negative operands.
        int result = calculator.add(-2, -3);
        assertEquals(-5, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_mixed_sign_positive_first() {
        // If the method add is called with a positive integer as the first argument and a negative integer as the second (e.g., 5 and -3), then it must return the correct arithmetic sum (2). This tests addition involving mixed signs where the positive operand is larger in magnitude.
        int result = calculator.add(5, -3);
        assertEquals(2, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_mixed_sign_negative_first() {
        // If the method add is called with a negative integer as the first argument and a positive integer as the second (e.g., -5 and 3), then it must return the correct arithmetic sum (-2). This tests addition involving mixed signs where the negative operand is larger in magnitude.
        int result = calculator.add(-5, 3);
        assertEquals(-2, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_zero_to_positive() {
        // If the method add is called with a positive integer and zero (e.g., 5 and 0), then it must return the positive integer unchanged (5). This verifies the identity property of addition with zero.
        int result = calculator.add(5, 0);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_zero_to_negative() {
        // If the method add is called with a negative integer and zero (e.g., -5 and 0), then it must return the negative integer unchanged (-5). This verifies the identity property of addition with zero for negative numbers.
        int result = calculator.add(-5, 0);
        assertEquals(-5, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_zero_to_zero() {
        // If the method add is called with zero and zero (0 and 0), then it must return 0. This verifies the base case of adding two zeros.
        int result = calculator.add(0, 0);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_max_value_overflow() {
        // If the method add is called with Integer.MAX_VALUE and 1, then it must return Integer.MIN_VALUE. This verifies that integer overflow wraps around correctly according to standard Java int arithmetic, as no exception is specified in the documentation.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_min_value_underflow() {
        // If the method add is called with Integer.MIN_VALUE and -1, then it must return Integer.MAX_VALUE. This verifies that integer underflow wraps around correctly according to standard Java int arithmetic, as no exception is specified in the documentation.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, -1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_extreme_values() {
        // If the method add is called with Integer.MAX_VALUE and Integer.MIN_VALUE, then it must return -1. This verifies correct arithmetic handling at the boundaries of the integer range.
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE);
        assertEquals(-1, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_commutative_property() {
        // If the method add is called with arguments (a, b) and separately with (b, a) for any valid integers (e.g., 10 and 20), then both calls must return the same result (30). This verifies that the operation is commutative, a fundamental property of addition.
        int result1 = calculator.add(10, 20);
        int result2 = calculator.add(20, 10);
        assertEquals(result1, result2);
        assertEquals(30, result1);
    }

package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here

public class CalculatorTest {
    private final Calculator calculator = new Calculator();

    @Test
    public void testSubtractPositiveIntegers() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=10 and b=3, then it should return 7. This tests the basic functionality of subtracting two positive integers.
        assertEquals(7, calculator.subtract(10, 3));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractNegativeIntegers() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=-5 and b=-2, then it should return -3. This tests the functionality of subtracting two negative integers.
        assertEquals(-3, calculator.subtract(-5, -2));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractNegativeFromPositive() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=-5 and b=3, then it should return -8. This tests the functionality when the first operand is negative and the second is positive.
        assertEquals(-8, calculator.subtract(-5, 3));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractNegativeFromNegative() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=5 and b=-3, then it should return 8. This tests the functionality when the first operand is positive and the second is negative (equivalent to addition).
        assertEquals(8, calculator.subtract(5, -3));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractZeroFromNumber() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=5 and b=0, then it should return 5. This tests the identity property where subtracting zero from a number leaves it unchanged.
        assertEquals(5, calculator.subtract(5, 0));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractNumberFromZero() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=0 and b=5, then it should return -5. This tests the property where subtracting a number from zero results in the negation of that number.
        assertEquals(-5, calculator.subtract(0, 5));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractIdenticalNumbers() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=7 and b=7, then it should return 0. This tests the property that subtracting a number from itself results in zero.
        assertEquals(0, calculator.subtract(7, 7));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractZeroFromZero() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=0 and b=0, then it should return 0. This tests the edge case where both operands are zero.
        assertEquals(0, calculator.subtract(0, 0));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractZeroFromMaxValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=0, then it should return Integer.MAX_VALUE. This tests the boundary condition at the maximum integer value.
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 0));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractZeroFromMinValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=0, then it should return Integer.MIN_VALUE. This tests the boundary condition at the minimum integer value.
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 0));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractOneFromMaxValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=1, then it should return 2147483646. This tests arithmetic just below the maximum integer value.
        assertEquals(2147483646, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractOneFromMinValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=1, then it should return Integer.MAX_VALUE. This tests the underflow behavior where subtracting 1 from the minimum integer value wraps around to the maximum integer value due to two's complement arithmetic.
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 1));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractMinValueFromMaxValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=Integer.MIN_VALUE, then it should return -1. This tests the overflow behavior where the result exceeds the maximum integer value and wraps around.
        assertEquals(-1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractMaxValueFromMinValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=Integer.MAX_VALUE, then it should return 1. This tests the underflow behavior where the result is less than the minimum integer value and wraps around.
        assertEquals(1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractMaxValueFromMaxValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=Integer.MAX_VALUE, then it should return 0. This tests the case where identical maximum values are subtracted.
        assertEquals(0, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE));
    }


    @Test
    public void testSubtractMinValueFromMinValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=Integer.MIN_VALUE, then it should return 0. This tests the case where identical minimum values are subtracted.
        assertEquals(0, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }

package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here
public class CalculatorTest {

    private final Calculator calculator = new Calculator();

    @Test
    public void testSubtractPositiveIntegers() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=10 and b=3, then it should return 7. This tests the basic functionality of subtracting two positive integers.
        assertEquals(7, calculator.subtract(10, 3));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractNegativeIntegers() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=-5 and b=-2, then it should return -3. This tests the functionality of subtracting two negative integers.
        assertEquals(-3, calculator.subtract(-5, -2));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractNegativeFromPositive() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=-5 and b=3, then it should return -8. This tests the functionality when the first operand is negative and the second is positive.
        assertEquals(-8, calculator.subtract(-5, 3));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractNegativeFromNegative() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=5 and b=-3, then it should return 8. This tests the functionality when the first operand is positive and the second is negative (equivalent to addition).
        assertEquals(8, calculator.subtract(5, -3));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractZeroFromNumber() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=5 and b=0, then it should return 5. This tests the identity property where subtracting zero from a number leaves it unchanged.
        assertEquals(5, calculator.subtract(5, 0));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractNumberFromZero() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=0 and b=5, then it should return -5. This tests the property where subtracting a number from zero results in the negation of that number.
        assertEquals(-5, calculator.subtract(0, 5));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractIdenticalNumbers() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=7 and b=7, then it should return 0. This tests the property that subtracting a number from itself results in zero.
        assertEquals(0, calculator.subtract(7, 7));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractZeroFromZero() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=0 and b=0, then it should return 0. This tests the edge case where both operands are zero.
        assertEquals(0, calculator.subtract(0, 0));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractZeroFromMaxValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=0, then it should return Integer.MAX_VALUE. This tests the boundary condition at the maximum integer value.
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 0));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractZeroFromMinValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=0, then it should return Integer.MIN_VALUE. This tests the boundary condition at the minimum integer value.
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 0));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractOneFromMaxValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=1, then it should return 2147483646. This tests arithmetic just below the maximum integer value.
        assertEquals(2147483646, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractOneFromMinValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=1, then it should return Integer.MAX_VALUE. This tests the underflow behavior where subtracting 1 from the minimum integer value wraps around to the maximum integer value due to two's complement arithmetic.
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 1));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractMinValueFromMaxValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=Integer.MIN_VALUE, then it should return -1. This tests the overflow behavior where the result exceeds the maximum integer value and wraps around.
        assertEquals(-1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractMaxValueFromMinValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=Integer.MAX_VALUE, then it should return 1. This tests the underflow behavior where the result is less than the minimum integer value and wraps around.
        assertEquals(1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractMaxValueFromMaxValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MAX_VALUE and b=Integer.MAX_VALUE, then it should return 0. This tests the case where identical maximum values are subtracted.
        assertEquals(0, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE));
    }

    @Test
    public void testSubtractMinValueFromMinValue() {
        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=Integer.MIN_VALUE, then it should return 0. This tests the case where identical minimum values are subtracted.
        assertEquals(0, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
    }
}

    91	        // If the method subtract is called with a=Integer.MIN_VALUE and b=1, then it should return Integer.MAX_VALUE. This tests the underflow behavior where subtracting 1 from the minimum integer value wraps around to the maximum integer value due to two's complement arithmetic.
    92	        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 1));
    93	    }
    94

    97	    public void testSubtractMinValueFromMaxValue() {

    99	        assertEquals(-1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
   100	    }

   106	        assertEquals(1, calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE));
   107	    }

public class Calculator {

    /**

    52	        
    53	        // Capture System.out and System.err to verify no output is produced
    54	        ByteArrayOutputStream outContent = new ByteArrayOutputStream();
    55	        ByteArrayOutputStream errContent = new ByteArrayOutputStream();
    56	        
    57	        PrintStream originalOut = System.out;
    58	        PrintStream originalErr = System.err;
    59	        
    60	        try {
    61	            System.setOut(new PrintStream(outContent));
    62	            System.setErr(new PrintStream(errContent));
    63	            
    64	            Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
    65	            calculator.dummy2();
    66	            
    67	            // Assert that no output was written to System.out
    68	            assertEquals("", outContent.toString());
    69	            // Assert that no output was written to System.err
    70	            assertEquals("", errContent.toString());
    71	        } finally {
    72	            // Restore original streams
    73	            System.setOut(originalOut);
    74	            System.setErr(originalErr);
    75	        }
    76	    }
